[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you, doctor. I appreciate you taking the time to see me. You know, I've been debating coming in for a little while now, and I finally just decided I had to do it. It really comes down to this thing that keeps happening over at the local museum. I spend a fair bit of time down there these days, just staying busy since I'm not working, and it’s a really good group of folks. We put on these community events and whatnot. \n\nBut what brings me in is... well, people at the museum will be standing right in front of me, talking right to me about, say, the plans for the next art walk we're putting together. And I’ll be looking right at them, making eye contact, trying to be polite, you know? I'm nodding along. But then, all of a sudden, I realize I completely missed the entire middle part of what they just said. It's like my brain just went somewhere else entirely, even though my eyes are still pointed right at their face. And then they pause, waiting for me to chime in, and I have to stop them—and it's awfully embarrassing—but I have to ask them to run it by me again. It just makes me feel like I'm being terribly rude, even though I don't mean to be. I just don't understand why I can't just listen and stay tuned in when someone is standing right there talking to me.\n\nDoctor: During these moments when you realize you've missed part of the conversation, can you recall what was going through your mind—were you thinking about something else, or does it feel like a total blank where you weren't aware of anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the strange part of it, doctor, and honestly, it's something I've tried to figure out myself because it bothers me so much. You'd think if I wasn't listening to them, I'd be thinking about something important, right? Like maybe I'd be planning what I'm going to have for dinner, or worrying about the electric bill, or remembering something I forgot to do at the house. But it's not like that. It's not like I'm sitting there having a whole other conversation in my head about something specific. \n\nIt's more like... well, it's just this fuzzy drift. I don't really know how else to describe it. One second I'm listening to Mary talk about the new display cases for the pottery exhibit—and Mary, bless her heart, she does tend to go on a bit, but she means well—and the next thing I know, I'm just sort of looking at the pattern on her scarf, or maybe I'm noticing a smudge on the glass behind her, and my brain just sort of... floats off. But it's not a deep thought. It's not like I'm pondering the meaning of life or anything. It's just a blank, really. A drift. And then she stops talking, and I snap back, and I realize I have absolutely no idea what she said for the last two minutes. It's not a total blackout, like I passed out, but it's just... nothing of substance was happening up there. I just wasn't present. And it's so frustrating because I *want* to hear what she's saying, I really do. I just can't seem to keep my brain tethered to the conversation.\n\nDoctor: When one of these episodes happens, about how many seconds or minutes does it usually last, and does it seem to start and stop very suddenly, almost like a switch flipping, or does your attention drift away and come back more gradually?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, that’s a tough one to answer, doctor. You know, I’ve asked myself that very same thing, tried to really pin it down, but it’s awfully hard to put a number on it because, well... I’m not really present to check my watch, am I? I mean, sometimes I think it might just be a few seconds, just a quick little blip. But then other times, I’ll snap back and realize they’ve moved on to a completely different topic. \n\nLike just last Tuesday, we were talking about the schedule for the volunteer shifts at the front desk—which, let me tell you, is a whole ordeal in itself, trying to get everyone to agree on times, especially with the holidays coming up and folks traveling to see their grandkids—and anyway, I drifted off. When I finally tuned back in, Mary and the others were talking about the catering budget for the opening reception! So that had to be at least a few minutes, right? To get from scheduling all the way over to the food? But I couldn't tell you for sure. It’s just a chunk of lost time. \n\nAnd as for how it starts and stops... a switch flipping, or gradual? I really couldn’t tell you that either, to be perfectly honest. It’s not like I feel myself slipping away. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, here I go, my attention is leaving now.\" It just happens. I’m listening, and then suddenly I’m not. But I only realize I wasn't listening when I finally snap back to reality. The snapping back—that part feels a little sudden, I suppose, kind of like waking up from a daydream when someone says your name or drops a book. But the drifting away? I have absolutely no idea. It’s completely stealthy. So I really can't give you a precise measurement on the time or the exact mechanics of how it begins, as much as I wish I could. I’ve tried to pay attention to it, to catch myself in the act, but you can't really pay attention to not paying attention, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, have you or anyone else noticed any physical signs happening on your face or body—like eye blinking or fluttering, lip movements, or a fixed stare—while you're 'drifted off'?\n\nPatient: Eye blinking or fluttering? No, no, doctor, nothing like that at all. I mean... if I was standing there fluttering my eyelids or doing something strange with my lips while people were talking to me, I am absolutely certain someone would have brought it to my attention by now. \n\nEspecially Mary. I know I keep bringing her up, but you have to understand the dynamic we have over there. Mary is the kind of person who notices *everything*. Just last month, I switched from my old wire-rim glasses to these plastic ones—my daughter said they made me look a bit younger, though I'm not sure I believe her, but they were on a two-for-one sale at the optometrist down on Main Street, so I figured why not—and Mary noticed the very second I walked through the museum doors. She didn't miss a beat. So, if my face was twitching or my eyes were fluttering, she’d be the absolute first to say, 'Are you feeling quite alright?' or something to that effect. She wouldn't just let that slide. \n\nBut no one has ever mentioned anything like that to me. And I don't feel anything happening on my face, either. As far as I know, I'm just standing there, nodding along like I normally would. I suppose you could say I have a stare, because like I mentioned, I am looking right into their eyes, trying my best to be a good listener, but it's not a... well, it's not some strange, frozen, medical kind of stare, if you catch my drift. It's just my regular, polite listening face. I'm just looking at them, probably blinking normally like anyone else would in a conversation. There's no weird lip movements or anything physical like that happening. It’s exactly like my old shop teacher used to say—the lights are on, but nobody's home. The outside looks completely normal, it's just on the inside where the connection just sort of... drops out.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, about how many times does this kind of 'drifting off' happen to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, how many times a day... well, now, that really depends on the day and how busy we are down at the museum. On a quiet afternoon when it's just me and maybe one or two visitors wandering through the exhibits, maybe it only happens once or twice. But on a busy day? Oh boy. \n\nIn fact, that's really what brings me in to see you in the first place, because it’s getting to be a real problem for me. You see, people at the museum will be talking right to me—especially lately, with everyone in a tizzy about the next art walk we're hosting. The art walk is a huge event for our little town, bringing in folks from three counties over, so there are a million little details to sort out with the local vendors and the historical society. So folks will be standing right in front of me, explaining something important about the setup or the flyers. And I’ll be looking right at them, making perfect eye contact, trying to be a good, attentive volunteer, you know? \n\nBut then, suddenly, I realize I completely missed the middle of what they said. I caught the beginning, and I caught the end, but the actual meat of the conversation just vanished into thin air. And then I have to swallow my pride, stop them, and say, 'I'm so sorry, could you run that by me again?' \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, doctor, it really is. Having to ask a grown adult to repeat themselves when you were looking right at them the whole time... it just makes you feel foolish, like they must think I don't care about what they're saying, which couldn't be further from the truth! So, on a day when we're doing heavy planning for the art walk and I'm talking to a lot of people, it could happen, gosh, maybe five, six, even seven times? It really just depends on how many conversations I'm having that day, but it's enough to make me feel like I'm losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back over your life—say to your school years or early adulthood—did you ever have trouble with your mind drifting off like this before, or is this something that has only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, no, doctor, this isn't a new thing at all. Not by a long shot. If I really sit back and think about it, going all the way back to when I was just a little boy... well, it's always been this way, hasn't it? \n\nI remember my old grade school report cards—you know, the thick paper ones they used to hand out in those little manila envelopes, where the teacher had to write everything out by hand in that perfect cursive they used to teach back in the day? Well, my mother kept all of those in a shoebox, and I remember looking through them years later. Every single one of them, from Mrs. Gable in second grade all the way up, they all said the exact same things. Down in the little comments section at the bottom, they'd always write \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" Always that same tune. I was a good kid, you understand, I never caused any trouble or threw spitballs or anything like that, but my mind would just wander right out the classroom window. \n\nAnd my poor mother, bless her heart, she knew it too. Before I was twelve years old, she used to have to sit right there beside me at the kitchen table every single afternoon just to keep me on track with my homework. If she got up for even a second to stir the soup on the stove or answer the telephone—we had one of those long coiled cords that stretched all the way across the kitchen, you remember those?—well, I'd be off in my own little world again, staring at the grain of the wood on the table or watching a bug on the windowsill instead of doing my arithmetic. She had to sit right there and tap the paper to bring me back. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, it's been happening for just about as long as I can remember. It's just that now, with all the responsibilities at the museum and having to coordinate with so many different people for the art walk, it feels like it's getting in the way a whole lot more than it used to when I was just a kid staring out a window.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the museum, do you notice this same kind of 'drifting off' or trouble staying focused in other parts of your life—like when you're reading, watching TV, driving, or handling things at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, yes... yes, I certainly do. It's not just when I'm standing there talking to people, if that's what you mean. It happens right in my own living room, too, especially when I'm trying to get some reading done. \n\nYou see, I like to bring home some of the materials from the museum to study up on—I take my volunteering very seriously, and I like to know the background of the pieces we're showing. So, I'll get myself all set up at home. I have this old blue recliner in the corner of the living room, had it for maybe fifteen years now, most comfortable chair in the house. I'll make myself a cup of decaf coffee, put on those new plastic glasses I was telling you about, and I'll sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes for the upcoming exhibits. \n\nAnd I always start out just fine! I'll read the first page or so, feeling very productive, really taking in the history of the local painters or whatever it is I'm supposed to be reviewing. But then... well, after just a few minutes, that same sort of drifting thing happens. My eyes keep moving over the page, going line by line, left to right, just exactly like they're supposed to. If you were sitting right there on the sofa across from me, you'd think I was the most dedicated reader in the world, completely engrossed in the material. \n\nBut my mind? My mind has completely wandered off. It’s out the door, down the street, thinking about whether I need to clean the gutters this weekend, or what Mary said about the vendor permits for the art walk, or if I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner. And then, all of a sudden, I'll get to the very bottom of the page, and it hits me that I haven't comprehended a single word of what I just 'read' for the last five minutes. The lights were on, but nobody was home, just like with the conversations. \n\nAnd then, of course, I have to stop, let out a big sigh, find where I lost the thread, and read the exact same part all over again. Sometimes I have to read the exact same paragraph three or four times before the words actually stick in my brain and make any sense. It turns a simple ten-minute reading task into a whole afternoon ordeal, I tell you. It's just terribly frustrating to spend all that time looking at a page and have absolutely nothing to show for it.\n\nDoctor: Besides this drifting off, do you often find yourself losing track of personal items like keys or glasses, forgetting appointments, or struggling to keep things organized in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, my stars, yes. It's funny you mention that, because... well, maybe not my glasses so much, since I usually keep them on that little chain around my neck now just to be safe, but with other things? Oh, it's a daily struggle, doctor, it really is. Especially down at the museum with all this art walk business going on. \n\nJust the other day—no, wait, it was Tuesday, because Tuesday is when Mary comes in to do the dusting in the East Wing. Anyway, I had the digital camera out to take pictures of the new display cases for the local paper. We like to send them a little press release, you see, to drum up interest. So I took the battery out to charge it, and I thought to myself, 'I'll just set this down somewhere safe so I know exactly where it is.' And while I was at it, I had the new volunteer name tags in my hand, and this big, thick manila envelope—the one with all the sign-up sheets for the art walk vendors. \n\nWell, I set them all down together. Somewhere safe. And wouldn't you know it, an hour later when I needed them, I couldn't for the life of me remember where 'safe' was. This happens all the time lately. I’m always putting down the camera battery, the name tags, or that envelope with the sign-up sheets somewhere safe, and then I end up spending half the morning just retracing my steps to find them! \n\nI'll walk back to the front desk, and I'll look under the ledger. Then I'll go check the little kitchenette we have in the back—did I leave them next to the coffee percolator? No. Then I'll go check the coat closet, check my jacket pockets, ask Mary if she moved them while she was dusting... it turns into a whole grand production. I swear I spend more time walking in circles looking for the things I just put down than I do actually doing my volunteer work. You'd think if I specifically told myself I was putting something in a safe spot, my brain would hold onto where that spot actually is, but it just slips right out of my head like water through a sieve. It drives me absolutely crazy, because I know I had them right in my hand not five minutes prior!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0"], "note": "Lifelong history since childhood of inattention, distractibility, forgetfulness, and losing items, occurring across multiple settings (work, home, reading), without motor hyperactivity signs or absence-seizure features (no automatisms, no true unresponsiveness, gradual/variable duration inconsistent with seizure activity). This supports a predominantly inattentive presentation of ADHD, consistent with F90.0. No new differential diagnoses introduced this turn; further findings reinforce rather than rule out this candidate.", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, no, doctor. Not at all. That doesn't sound like me in the slightest. If anything, I'm quite the opposite, really. I'm a very calm person, physically speaking, you know? 

I don't have that sort of nervous energy some folks seem to carry around with them. You see people tapping their feet or drumming their fingers on the table—take my brother-in-law, Frank, for instance. Frank can't sit still for two minutes without jiggling his knee or pacing around the room looking for something to fiddle with. But me? No, I can sit perfectly still in that old blue recliner of mine for hours on end. The problem isn't my body wanting to get up and run a marathon, it's just my brain that decides to go for a walk, if you catch my drift. 

And as for interrupting people or blurting things out... well, I was raised with very strict manners. My mother, the same one who used to sit with me at the kitchen table to do my arithmetic, she would have tanned my hide if I ever interrupted someone who was speaking. So I've always prided myself on being a very polite conversationalist. Even down at the museum, when Mary—she's the one who does the dusting on Tuesdays, I think I mentioned her—when she gets to talking about her grandchildren, she can go on for twenty minutes straight without taking a single breath. And I just stand there, hands in my pockets, letting her finish her thought. Now, I might completely drift off and start thinking about whether we need to order more halogen lightbulbs for the display cases, but I would never dream of actually cutting her off or jumping in. 

And making quick decisions? Oh, my stars, no. If anything, I take far too long to make up my mind about things. Just last month, when we were picking out the caterer for the art walk reception, the board wanted me to just pick someone from the local directory and be done with it. But I spent... oh, it must have been three solid weeks, looking over every single menu they sent over, comparing the prices of the little cheese tartlets versus the stuffed mushrooms, checking references... I like to weigh all my options, turn things over in my mind, really look at a situation from every possible angle before I commit to a course of action. 

So, no, jumping the gun or acting without thinking... that's just not in my nature at all. I'm very slow and steady. It's just the focusing part, the holding onto the thoughts I'm supposed to be having, that gives me all this grief.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. To start, can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, thank you, doctor. I appreciate you taking the time to see me. You know, I've been debating coming in for a little while now, and I finally just decided I had to do it. It really comes down to this thing that keeps happening over at the local museum. I spend a fair bit of time down there these days, just staying busy since I'm not working, and it’s a really good group of folks. We put on these community events and whatnot. \n\nBut what brings me in is... well, people at the museum will be standing right in front of me, talking right to me about, say, the plans for the next art walk we're putting together. And I’ll be looking right at them, making eye contact, trying to be polite, you know? I'm nodding along. But then, all of a sudden, I realize I completely missed the entire middle part of what they just said. It's like my brain just went somewhere else entirely, even though my eyes are still pointed right at their face. And then they pause, waiting for me to chime in, and I have to stop them—and it's awfully embarrassing—but I have to ask them to run it by me again. It just makes me feel like I'm being terribly rude, even though I don't mean to be. I just don't understand why I can't just listen and stay tuned in when someone is standing right there talking to me.\n\nDoctor: During these moments when you realize you've missed part of the conversation, can you recall what was going through your mind—were you thinking about something else, or does it feel like a total blank where you weren't aware of anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the strange part of it, doctor, and honestly, it's something I've tried to figure out myself because it bothers me so much. You'd think if I wasn't listening to them, I'd be thinking about something important, right? Like maybe I'd be planning what I'm going to have for dinner, or worrying about the electric bill, or remembering something I forgot to do at the house. But it's not like that. It's not like I'm sitting there having a whole other conversation in my head about something specific. \n\nIt's more like... well, it's just this fuzzy drift. I don't really know how else to describe it. One second I'm listening to Mary talk about the new display cases for the pottery exhibit—and Mary, bless her heart, she does tend to go on a bit, but she means well—and the next thing I know, I'm just sort of looking at the pattern on her scarf, or maybe I'm noticing a smudge on the glass behind her, and my brain just sort of... floats off. But it's not a deep thought. It's not like I'm pondering the meaning of life or anything. It's just a blank, really. A drift. And then she stops talking, and I snap back, and I realize I have absolutely no idea what she said for the last two minutes. It's not a total blackout, like I passed out, but it's just... nothing of substance was happening up there. I just wasn't present. And it's so frustrating because I *want* to hear what she's saying, I really do. I just can't seem to keep my brain tethered to the conversation.\n\nDoctor: When one of these episodes happens, about how many seconds or minutes does it usually last, and does it seem to start and stop very suddenly, almost like a switch flipping, or does your attention drift away and come back more gradually?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, that’s a tough one to answer, doctor. You know, I’ve asked myself that very same thing, tried to really pin it down, but it’s awfully hard to put a number on it because, well... I’m not really present to check my watch, am I? I mean, sometimes I think it might just be a few seconds, just a quick little blip. But then other times, I’ll snap back and realize they’ve moved on to a completely different topic. \n\nLike just last Tuesday, we were talking about the schedule for the volunteer shifts at the front desk—which, let me tell you, is a whole ordeal in itself, trying to get everyone to agree on times, especially with the holidays coming up and folks traveling to see their grandkids—and anyway, I drifted off. When I finally tuned back in, Mary and the others were talking about the catering budget for the opening reception! So that had to be at least a few minutes, right? To get from scheduling all the way over to the food? But I couldn't tell you for sure. It’s just a chunk of lost time. \n\nAnd as for how it starts and stops... a switch flipping, or gradual? I really couldn’t tell you that either, to be perfectly honest. It’s not like I feel myself slipping away. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, here I go, my attention is leaving now.\" It just happens. I’m listening, and then suddenly I’m not. But I only realize I wasn't listening when I finally snap back to reality. The snapping back—that part feels a little sudden, I suppose, kind of like waking up from a daydream when someone says your name or drops a book. But the drifting away? I have absolutely no idea. It’s completely stealthy. So I really can't give you a precise measurement on the time or the exact mechanics of how it begins, as much as I wish I could. I’ve tried to pay attention to it, to catch myself in the act, but you can't really pay attention to not paying attention, if that makes any sense.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, have you or anyone else noticed any physical signs happening on your face or body—like eye blinking or fluttering, lip movements, or a fixed stare—while you're 'drifted off'?\n\nPatient: Eye blinking or fluttering? No, no, doctor, nothing like that at all. I mean... if I was standing there fluttering my eyelids or doing something strange with my lips while people were talking to me, I am absolutely certain someone would have brought it to my attention by now. \n\nEspecially Mary. I know I keep bringing her up, but you have to understand the dynamic we have over there. Mary is the kind of person who notices *everything*. Just last month, I switched from my old wire-rim glasses to these plastic ones—my daughter said they made me look a bit younger, though I'm not sure I believe her, but they were on a two-for-one sale at the optometrist down on Main Street, so I figured why not—and Mary noticed the very second I walked through the museum doors. She didn't miss a beat. So, if my face was twitching or my eyes were fluttering, she’d be the absolute first to say, 'Are you feeling quite alright?' or something to that effect. She wouldn't just let that slide. \n\nBut no one has ever mentioned anything like that to me. And I don't feel anything happening on my face, either. As far as I know, I'm just standing there, nodding along like I normally would. I suppose you could say I have a stare, because like I mentioned, I am looking right into their eyes, trying my best to be a good listener, but it's not a... well, it's not some strange, frozen, medical kind of stare, if you catch my drift. It's just my regular, polite listening face. I'm just looking at them, probably blinking normally like anyone else would in a conversation. There's no weird lip movements or anything physical like that happening. It’s exactly like my old shop teacher used to say—the lights are on, but nobody's home. The outside looks completely normal, it's just on the inside where the connection just sort of... drops out.\n\nDoctor: On a typical day, about how many times does this kind of 'drifting off' happen to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, how many times a day... well, now, that really depends on the day and how busy we are down at the museum. On a quiet afternoon when it's just me and maybe one or two visitors wandering through the exhibits, maybe it only happens once or twice. But on a busy day? Oh boy. \n\nIn fact, that's really what brings me in to see you in the first place, because it’s getting to be a real problem for me. You see, people at the museum will be talking right to me—especially lately, with everyone in a tizzy about the next art walk we're hosting. The art walk is a huge event for our little town, bringing in folks from three counties over, so there are a million little details to sort out with the local vendors and the historical society. So folks will be standing right in front of me, explaining something important about the setup or the flyers. And I’ll be looking right at them, making perfect eye contact, trying to be a good, attentive volunteer, you know? \n\nBut then, suddenly, I realize I completely missed the middle of what they said. I caught the beginning, and I caught the end, but the actual meat of the conversation just vanished into thin air. And then I have to swallow my pride, stop them, and say, 'I'm so sorry, could you run that by me again?' \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, doctor, it really is. Having to ask a grown adult to repeat themselves when you were looking right at them the whole time... it just makes you feel foolish, like they must think I don't care about what they're saying, which couldn't be further from the truth! So, on a day when we're doing heavy planning for the art walk and I'm talking to a lot of people, it could happen, gosh, maybe five, six, even seven times? It really just depends on how many conversations I'm having that day, but it's enough to make me feel like I'm losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back over your life—say to your school years or early adulthood—did you ever have trouble with your mind drifting off like this before, or is this something that has only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, no, doctor, this isn't a new thing at all. Not by a long shot. If I really sit back and think about it, going all the way back to when I was just a little boy... well, it's always been this way, hasn't it? \n\nI remember my old grade school report cards—you know, the thick paper ones they used to hand out in those little manila envelopes, where the teacher had to write everything out by hand in that perfect cursive they used to teach back in the day? Well, my mother kept all of those in a shoebox, and I remember looking through them years later. Every single one of them, from Mrs. Gable in second grade all the way up, they all said the exact same things. Down in the little comments section at the bottom, they'd always write \"daydreams\" or \"needs reminders to finish work.\" Always that same tune. I was a good kid, you understand, I never caused any trouble or threw spitballs or anything like that, but my mind would just wander right out the classroom window. \n\nAnd my poor mother, bless her heart, she knew it too. Before I was twelve years old, she used to have to sit right there beside me at the kitchen table every single afternoon just to keep me on track with my homework. If she got up for even a second to stir the soup on the stove or answer the telephone—we had one of those long coiled cords that stretched all the way across the kitchen, you remember those?—well, I'd be off in my own little world again, staring at the grain of the wood on the table or watching a bug on the windowsill instead of doing my arithmetic. She had to sit right there and tap the paper to bring me back. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question, it's been happening for just about as long as I can remember. It's just that now, with all the responsibilities at the museum and having to coordinate with so many different people for the art walk, it feels like it's getting in the way a whole lot more than it used to when I was just a kid staring out a window.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the museum, do you notice this same kind of 'drifting off' or trouble staying focused in other parts of your life—like when you're reading, watching TV, driving, or handling things at home?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, yes... yes, I certainly do. It's not just when I'm standing there talking to people, if that's what you mean. It happens right in my own living room, too, especially when I'm trying to get some reading done. \n\nYou see, I like to bring home some of the materials from the museum to study up on—I take my volunteering very seriously, and I like to know the background of the pieces we're showing. So, I'll get myself all set up at home. I have this old blue recliner in the corner of the living room, had it for maybe fifteen years now, most comfortable chair in the house. I'll make myself a cup of decaf coffee, put on those new plastic glasses I was telling you about, and I'll sit down with a gallery packet or a set of notes for the upcoming exhibits. \n\nAnd I always start out just fine! I'll read the first page or so, feeling very productive, really taking in the history of the local painters or whatever it is I'm supposed to be reviewing. But then... well, after just a few minutes, that same sort of drifting thing happens. My eyes keep moving over the page, going line by line, left to right, just exactly like they're supposed to. If you were sitting right there on the sofa across from me, you'd think I was the most dedicated reader in the world, completely engrossed in the material. \n\nBut my mind? My mind has completely wandered off. It’s out the door, down the street, thinking about whether I need to clean the gutters this weekend, or what Mary said about the vendor permits for the art walk, or if I remembered to take the chicken out of the freezer for dinner. And then, all of a sudden, I'll get to the very bottom of the page, and it hits me that I haven't comprehended a single word of what I just 'read' for the last five minutes. The lights were on, but nobody was home, just like with the conversations. \n\nAnd then, of course, I have to stop, let out a big sigh, find where I lost the thread, and read the exact same part all over again. Sometimes I have to read the exact same paragraph three or four times before the words actually stick in my brain and make any sense. It turns a simple ten-minute reading task into a whole afternoon ordeal, I tell you. It's just terribly frustrating to spend all that time looking at a page and have absolutely nothing to show for it.\n\nDoctor: Besides this drifting off, do you often find yourself losing track of personal items like keys or glasses, forgetting appointments, or struggling to keep things organized in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Oh, my stars, yes. It's funny you mention that, because... well, maybe not my glasses so much, since I usually keep them on that little chain around my neck now just to be safe, but with other things? Oh, it's a daily struggle, doctor, it really is. Especially down at the museum with all this art walk business going on. \n\nJust the other day—no, wait, it was Tuesday, because Tuesday is when Mary comes in to do the dusting in the East Wing. Anyway, I had the digital camera out to take pictures of the new display cases for the local paper. We like to send them a little press release, you see, to drum up interest. So I took the battery out to charge it, and I thought to myself, 'I'll just set this down somewhere safe so I know exactly where it is.' And while I was at it, I had the new volunteer name tags in my hand, and this big, thick manila envelope—the one with all the sign-up sheets for the art walk vendors. \n\nWell, I set them all down together. Somewhere safe. And wouldn't you know it, an hour later when I needed them, I couldn't for the life of me remember where 'safe' was. This happens all the time lately. I’m always putting down the camera battery, the name tags, or that envelope with the sign-up sheets somewhere safe, and then I end up spending half the morning just retracing my steps to find them! \n\nI'll walk back to the front desk, and I'll look under the ledger. Then I'll go check the little kitchenette we have in the back—did I leave them next to the coffee percolator? No. Then I'll go check the coat closet, check my jacket pockets, ask Mary if she moved them while she was dusting... it turns into a whole grand production. I swear I spend more time walking in circles looking for the things I just put down than I do actually doing my volunteer work. You'd think if I specifically told myself I was putting something in a safe spot, my brain would hold onto where that spot actually is, but it just slips right out of my head like water through a sieve. It drives me absolutely crazy, because I know I had them right in my hand not five minutes prior!\n\nDoctor: Aside from the attention difficulties, do you notice yourself feeling restless, needing to fidget or move around, or acting impulsively—like interrupting others or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, doctor. Not at all. That doesn't sound like me in the slightest. If anything, I'm quite the opposite, really. I'm a very calm person, physically speaking, you know? \n\nI don't have that sort of nervous energy some folks seem to carry around with them. You see people tapping their feet or drumming their fingers on the table—take my brother-in-law, Frank, for instance. Frank can't sit still for two minutes without jiggling his knee or pacing around the room looking for something to fiddle with. But me? No, I can sit perfectly still in that old blue recliner of mine for hours on end. The problem isn't my body wanting to get up and run a marathon, it's just my brain that decides to go for a walk, if you catch my drift. \n\nAnd as for interrupting people or blurting things out... well, I was raised with very strict manners. My mother, the same one who used to sit with me at the kitchen table to do my arithmetic, she would have tanned my hide if I ever interrupted someone who was speaking. So I've always prided myself on being a very polite conversationalist. Even down at the museum, when Mary—she's the one who does the dusting on Tuesdays, I think I mentioned her—when she gets to talking about her grandchildren, she can go on for twenty minutes straight without taking a single breath. And I just stand there, hands in my pockets, letting her finish her thought. Now, I might completely drift off and start thinking about whether we need to order more halogen lightbulbs for the display cases, but I would never dream of actually cutting her off or jumping in. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, my stars, no. If anything, I take far too long to make up my mind about things. Just last month, when we were picking out the caterer for the art walk reception, the board wanted me to just pick someone from the local directory and be done with it. But I spent... oh, it must have been three solid weeks, looking over every single menu they sent over, comparing the prices of the little cheese tartlets versus the stuffed mushrooms, checking references... I like to weigh all my options, turn things over in my mind, really look at a situation from every possible angle before I commit to a course of action. \n\nSo, no, jumping the gun or acting without thinking... that's just not in my nature at all. I'm very slow and steady. It's just the focusing part, the holding onto the thoughts I'm supposed to be having, that gives me all this grief.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Frank can't sit still for two minutes without jiggling his knee or pacing around the room looking for something to fiddle with. But me? No, I can sit perfectly still in that old blue recliner of mine for hours on end. The problem isn't my body wanting to get up and run a marathon, it's just my brain that decides to go for a walk, if you catch my drift. \n\nAnd as for interrupting people or blurting things out... well, I was raised with very strict manners. My mother, the same one who used to sit with me at the kitchen table to do my arithmetic, she would have tanned my hide if I ever interrupted someone who was speaking. So I've always prided myself on being a very polite conversationalist. Even down at the museum, when Mary—she's the one who does the dusting on Tuesdays, I think I mentioned her—when she gets to talking about her grandchildren, she can go on for twenty minutes straight without taking a single breath. And I just stand there, hands in my pockets, letting her finish her thought. Now, I might completely drift off and start thinking about whether we need to order more halogen lightbulbs for the display cases, but I would never dream of actually cutting her off or jumping in. \n\nAnd making quick decisions? Oh, my stars, no. If anything, I take far too long to make up my mind about things. Just last month, when we were picking out the caterer for the art walk reception, the board wanted me to just pick someone from the local directory and be done with it. But I spent... oh, it must have been three solid weeks, looking over every single menu they sent over, comparing the prices of the little cheese tartlets versus the stuffed mushrooms, checking references... I like to weigh all my options, turn things over in my mind, really look at a situation from every possible angle before I commit to a course of action. \n\nSo, no, jumping the gun or acting without thinking... that's just not in my nature at all. I'm very slow and steady. It's just the focusing part, the holding onto the thoughts I'm supposed to be having, that gives me all this grief."
  }
]

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. I spend a fair bit of time down there these days, just staying busy since I'm not working, and it’s a really good group of folks. We put on these community events and whatnot. \n\nBut what brings me in is... well, people at the museum will be standing right in front of me, talking right to me about, say, the plans for the next art walk we're putting together. But then, all of a sudden, I realize I completely missed the entire middle part of what they just said. It's like my brain just went somewhere else entirely, even though my eyes are still pointed right at their face. And then they pause, waiting for me to chime in, and I have to stop them—and it's awfully embarrassing—but I have to ask them to run it by me again. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, that's the strange part of it, doctor, and honestly, it's something I've tried to figure out myself because it bothers me so much. You'd think if I wasn't listening to them, I'd be thinking about something important, right? Like maybe I'd be planning what I'm going to have for dinner, or worrying about the electric bill, or remembering something I forgot to do at the house. But it's not like that. It's not like I'm sitting there having a whole other conversation in my head about something specific. \n\nIt's more like... well, it's just this fuzzy drift. I don't really know how else to describe it. One second I'm listening to Mary talk about the new display cases for the pottery exhibit—and Mary, bless her heart, she does tend to go on a bit, but she means well—and the next thing I know, I'm just sort of looking at the pattern on her scarf, or maybe I'm noticing a smudge on the glass behind her, and my brain just sort of... floats off. But it's not a deep thought. It's not like I'm pondering the meaning of life or anything. It's just a blank, really. A drift. And then she stops talking, and I snap back, and I realize I have absolutely no idea what she said for the last two minutes. It's not a total blackout, like I passed out, but it's just... nothing of substance was happening up there. I just wasn't present. And it's so frustrating because I *want* to hear what she's saying, I really do. I just can't seem to keep my brain tethered to the conversation.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I mean, sometimes I think it might just be a few seconds, just a quick little blip. But then other times, I’ll snap back and realize they’ve moved on to a completely different topic. \n\nLike just last Tuesday, we were talking about the schedule for the volunteer shifts at the front desk—which, let me tell you, is a whole ordeal in itself, trying to get everyone to agree on times, especially with the holidays coming up and folks traveling to see their grandkids—and anyway, I drifted off. When I finally tuned back in, Mary and the others were talking about the catering budget for the opening reception! So that had to be at least a few minutes, right? To get from scheduling all the way over to the food? But I couldn't tell you for sure. It’s just a chunk of lost time. \n\nAnd as for how it starts and stops... a switch flipping, or gradual? I really couldn’t tell you that either, to be perfectly honest. It’s not like I feel myself slipping away. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, here I go, my attention is leaving now.\" It just happens. I’m listening, and then suddenly I’m not. But I only realize I wasn't listening when I finally snap back to reality. The snapping back—that part feels a little sudden, I suppose, kind of like waking up from a daydream when someone says your name or drops a book. But the drifting away? I have absolutely no idea. It’s completely stealthy. So I really can't give you a precise measurement on the time or the exact mechanics of how it begins, as much as I wish I could. I’ve tried to pay attention to it, to catch myself in the act, but you can't really pay attention to not paying attention, if that makes any sense.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. On a quiet afternoon when it's just me and maybe one or two visitors wandering through the exhibits, maybe it only happens once or twice. But on a busy day? Oh boy. \n\nIn fact, that's really what brings me in to see you in the first place, because it’s getting to be a real problem for me. You see, people at the museum will be talking right to me—especially lately, with everyone in a tizzy about the next art walk we're hosting. The art walk is a huge event for our little town, bringing in folks from three counties over, so there are a million little details to sort out with the local vendors and the historical society. So folks will be standing right in front of me, explaining something important about the setup or the flyers. And I’ll be looking right at them, making perfect eye contact, trying to be a good, attentive volunteer, you know? \n\nBut then, suddenly, I realize I completely missed the middle of what they said. I caught the beginning, and I caught the end, but the actual meat of the conversation just vanished into thin air. And then I have to swallow my pride, stop them, and say, 'I'm so sorry, could you run that by me again?' \n\nIt's terribly embarrassing, doctor, it really is. Having to ask a grown adult to repeat themselves when you were looking right at them the whole time... it just makes you feel foolish, like they must think I don't care about what they're saying, which couldn't be further from the truth! So, on a day when we're doing heavy planning for the art walk and I'm talking to a lot of people, it could happen, gosh, maybe five, six, even seven times? It really just depends on how many conversations I'm having that day, but it's enough to make me feel like I'm losing my grip on things.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
